The Spirit of the Lord is upon Me, because He has anointed Me; He has sent Me to preach good tidings unto the meek, to heal the brokenhearted, to proclaim liberty to the captives, and the opening of the prison to the blind; to proclaim the acceptable year of the Lord, and the day of recompense; to comfort all that mourn; to appoint unto them that mourn in Zion, to give unto them glory instead of ashes, the oil of gladness to them that mourn, the garment of glory instead of the spirit of heaviness; that they might be called generations of righteousness, the planting of the Lord unto glory. And they shall build the old wastes, they shall raise up the former desolations, and they shall repair the waste cities, the desolations of many generations. And strangers shall stand and feed your flocks, and the sons of the alien shall be your plowmen and your vinedressers. But ye shall be named the Priests of the Lord: men shall call you the Ministers of our God: ye shall eat the riches of the Gentiles, and in their glory shall ye be admired. For your double shame and confusion, their portion shall rejoice: therefore in their land they shall possess the double; everlasting joy shall be upon their head. For I the Lord love judgment, I hate robbery for unrighteousness; and I will direct their work in truth, and I will make an everlasting covenant with them. And their seed shall be known among the Gentiles, and their offspring among the people: all that see them shall acknowledge them, that they are the seed which the Lord has blessed, and with gladness they shall rejoice in the Lord.

*The Twelfth Reading (4 Kings 4:8–37):*[^460]  
*A Reading from the Fourth Book of Kings (Chapter 4):*  
And it fell on a day, that Elisha passed to Shunem, where was a great woman; and she constrained him to eat bread. And so it was, that as oft as he passed by, he turned in thither to eat bread. And she said unto her husband: "Behold now, I perceive that this is an holy man of God, which passes by us continually. Let us make a little chamber, I pray thee, on the wall; and let us set for him there a bed, and a table, and a stool, and a candlestick: and it shall be, when he comes to us, that he shall turn in thither." And it fell on a day, that he came thither, and he turned into the chamber, and lay there. And he said to Gehazi his servant: "Call this Shunammite." And when he had called her, she stood before him. And he said unto him: "Say now unto her: Behold, thou hast been careful for us with all this care; what is to be done for thee? wouldest thou be spoken for to the King, or to the captain of the host?" And she answered: "I dwell among mine own people in quietness." And he said: "What then is to be done for her?" And Gehazi his servant answered: "Truly she has no son, and her husband is old." And he said: "Call her." And when he had called her, she stood in the door. And Elisha said: "About this season, according to the time of life, thou shalt embrace a son." And she said: "Nay, my lord, thou man of God, do not lie unto thine handmaid." And the woman conceived, and bore a son at that season that Elisha had said unto her, according to the time of life. And when the child was grown, it fell on a day, that he went out to his father to the reapers. And he said unto his father: "My head, my head!" And he said to a lad: "Carry him to his mother." And when he had taken him, and brought him to his mother, he sat on her knees till noon, and then died. And she went up, and laid him on the bed of the man of God, and shut the door upon him, and went out. And she called unto her husband, and said: "Send me, I pray thee, one of the young men, and one of the asses, that I may run to the man of God, and come again." And he said: "Why wilt thou go to him today? it is neither new moon, nor sabbath." And she said: "It shall be well." Then she saddled an ass, and said to her servant: "Drive, and go forward; slack not thy riding for me, except I bid thee. Go, and proceed, and come unto the man of God to mount Carmel." And it came to pass, when the man of God saw her afar off, that he said to Gehazi his servant: "Behold, yonder is that Shunammite! Run now, I pray thee, to meet her, and say unto her: Is it well with thee? is it well with thy husband? is it well with the child?" And she answered: "It is well." And when she came to the man of God to the hill, she caught him by the feet: but Gehazi came near to thrust her away. And Elisha said: "Let her alone; for her soul is vexed within her: and the Lord has hid it from me, and has not told me." Then she said: "Did I desire a son of my lord? did I not say, Do not deceive me?" Then he said to Gehazi:

"Gird up thy loins, and take my staff in thine hand, and go thy way: if thou meet any man, salute him not; and if any salute thee, answer him not again: and lay my staff upon the face of the child." And the mother of the child said: "As the Lord lives, and as thy soul lives, I will not leave thee." And Elisha arose, and followed her. And Gehazi passed on before them, and laid the staff upon the face of the child; but there was neither voice, nor hearing. Therefore he went again to meet him, and told him, saying: "The child is not awaked." And when Elisha was come into the house, behold, the child was dead, and laid upon his bed. He went in therefore, and shut the door upon them twain, and prayed unto the Lord. And he went up, and lay upon the child, and put his mouth upon his mouth, and his eyes upon his eyes, and his hands upon his hands, and his shoulders upon his shoulders; and he stretched himself upon the child; and he compassed him, and the flesh of the child waxed warm. Then he returned, and walked in the house to and fro; and went up, and stretched himself upon him up to seven times: and the child opened his eyes. And Elisha cried unto Gehazi, and said: "Call this Shunammite." So he called her. And when she was come in unto him, Elisha said: "Take up thy son." Then she went in, and fell at his feet, and bowed herself to the ground, and took up her son, and went out.

*The Thirteenth Reading (Isaiah 63:11–64:5):*[^461]  
*The Prophecy of Isaiah (Chapter 63):*  
Thus says the Lord: "Where is He that brought them up out of the sea with the shepherd of His sheep? Where is He that put His Holy Spirit within them? That led them by the right hand of Moses with His glorious arm, dividing the water before them, to make Himself an everlasting name? That led them through the deep, as an horse through the wilderness, that they should not stumble? As cattle go down into the valley, the Spirit from the Lord descended and guided them: so didst Thou lead Thy people, to make Thyself a glorious name. Look down from heaven, O Lord, and behold from the habitation of Thy holiness and of Thy glory: where is Thy zeal and Thy strength, the sounding of Thy bowels and of Thy mercies toward us? For Thou hast pitied us, O Lord. Doubtless Thou art our Father, though Abraham be ignorant of us, and Israel acknowledge us not: but Thou, O Lord, art our Father, deliver us; from the beginning Thy name is upon us. Why hast Thou made us to err from Thy ways, O Lord, and hardened our heart from Thy fear? Return for Thy servants' sake, the tribes of Thine inheritance, that we may inherit even a little of Thy holy mountain: our adversaries have trodden down Thy sanctuary. We are become as from the beginning, when Thou didst not rule over us, neither was Thy name called upon us.  
*(Chapter 64):*  
If Thou wouldest rend the heavens, the mountains would tremble before Thee, and melt away! As wax melts before the fire, and the fire burns up Thine adversaries, and Thy name shall be made manifest among Thine adversaries: at Thy presence the nations shall be troubled! When Thou doest terrible things, the mountains shall tremble before Thee. For since the beginning of the world men have not heard, nor have our eyes seen any God besides Thee, and Thy works which Thou doest for them that wait for Thy mercy. For He shall meet them that work righteousness, and they shall remember Thy ways.

*The Fourteenth Reading (Jeremiah 31:31–34):*[^462]  
*The Prophecy of Jeremiah (Chapter 31):*  
Thus says the Lord: "Behold, the days come, that I will make a new covenant with the house of Israel, and with the house of Judah: not according to the covenant that I made with their fathers in the day that I took them by the hand to bring them out of the land of Egypt; which My covenant they broke, and I regarded them not, says the Lord. But this shall be the covenant that I will make with the house of Israel; After those days, says the Lord, giving I will put My laws into their mind, and write them in their hearts; and I will see them, and I will be to them a God, and they shall be to Me a people. And they shall not teach every man his neighbor, and every man his brother, saying: Know the Lord: for all shall know Me, from the least to the greatest of them, says the Lord: for I will be merciful to their unrighteousness, and their sins will I remember no more."

*The Fifteenth Reading (Daniel 3:1–88):*[^463]  
*The Prophecy of Daniel (Chapter 3):*  
In the eighteenth year, Nebuchadnezzar the king made an image of gold, whose height was threescore cubits, and the breadth thereof six cubits: he set it up in the plain of Dura, in the province of Babylon. Then Nebuchadnezzar the king sent to gather together the princes, the governors, and the captains, the judges, the treasurers, the counselors, the sheriffs, and all the rulers of the provinces, to come to the dedication of the image which Nebuchadnezzar the king had set up. Then the princes, governors, and captains, the judges, the treasurers, the counselors, the sheriffs, and all the rulers of the provinces, were gathered together unto the dedication of the image that Nebuchadnezzar the king had set up; and they stood before the image. Then an herald cried aloud: "To you it is commanded, O people, nations, and languages, that at what time ye hear the sound of the cornet, flute, harp, sackbut, psaltery, dulcimer, and all kinds of music, ye fall down and worship the golden image that Nebuchadnezzar the king has set up: and whoso falls not down and worships shall the same hour be cast into the midst of a burning fiery furnace." Therefore at that time, when all the people heard the sound of the cornet, flute, harp, sackbut, psaltery, and all kinds of music, all the people, the nations, and the languages, fell down and worshipped the golden image that Nebuchadnezzar the king had set up before them. Therefore at that time certain Chaldeans came near, and accused the Jews. They spoke and said to the king Nebuchadnezzar: "O king, live forever! Thou, O king, hast made a decree, that every man that shall hear the sound of the cornet, flute, harp, sackbut, psaltery, and dulcimer, and all kinds of music, and falls not down and worships the golden image which thou hast set up, shall be cast into the midst of a burning fiery furnace. There are certain Jews whom thou hast set over the affairs of the province of Babylon, Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego; these men, O king, have not regarded thy decree: they serve not thy gods, nor worship the golden image which thou hast set up." Then Nebuchadnezzar in his rage and fury commanded to bring Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego. Then they brought these men before the king. Nebuchadnezzar spoke and said unto them: "Is it true, O Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego, do not ye serve my gods, nor worship the golden image which I have set up? Now if ye be ready that at what time ye hear the sound of the cornet, flute, harp, sackbut, psaltery, and dulcimer, and all kinds of music, ye fall down and worship the image which I have made, well: but if ye worship not, ye shall be cast the same hour into the midst of a burning fiery furnace; and who is that God that shall deliver you out of my hands?" Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego, answered and said unto King Nebuchadnezzar: "We have no need to answer thee in this matter. For our God Whom we serve is able to deliver us from the burning fiery furnace, and He will deliver us out of thine hand, O king. But if not, be it known unto thee, O king, that we will not serve thy gods, nor worship the golden image which thou hast set up." Then was Nebuchadnezzar full of fury, and the form of his visage was changed against Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego: therefore he spoke, and commanded that they should heat the furnace seven times more than it was wont to be heated, until it burned to the uttermost. And he commanded the most mighty men that were in his army to bind Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego, and to cast them into the burning fiery furnace. Then these men were bound in their coats, their hosen, and their hats, and their other garments, and were cast into the midst of the burning fiery furnace. Therefore because the king's commandment was urgent, and the furnace exceeding hot, the flame of the fire slew those men that took up Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego. And these three men, Shadrach, Meshach, and Abednego, fell down bound into the midst of the burning fiery furnace. And they walked in the midst of the fire, singing and blessing the Lord.
And Azariah stood up with them, and prayed on this manner; and opening his mouth in the midst of the fire, he said:[^464]  
*The Prayer or Song of the Three Holy Children:*  
Blessed art Thou, O Lord God of our fathers: Thy name is worthy to be praised and glorified unto ages. For Thou art righteous in all the things that Thou hast done to us: yea, true are all Thy works, Thy ways are right, and all Thy judgments truth. In all the things that Thou hast brought upon us, and upon the holy city of our fathers, even Jerusalem, Thou hast executed true judgment: for according unto truth and judgment didst Thou bring all these things upon us because of our sins. For we have sinned and committed iniquity, departing from Thee; and we have transgressed in all things, neither have we obeyed Thy commandments, nor kept them, neither done as Thou hast commanded us, that it might go well with us. Therefore all that Thou hast brought upon us, and every thing that Thou hast done to us, Thou hast done in true judgment. And Thou didst deliver us into the hands of lawless enemies, most hateful forsakers of God, and to an unjust king, and the most wicked in all the world. And now we cannot open our mouths, we are become a shame and reproach to Thy servants; and to them that worship Thee. Yet deliver us not up wholly, for Thy name's sake, neither disannul Thou Thy covenant: and cause not Thy mercy to depart from us, for Thy beloved Abraham's sake, for Thy servant Isaac's sake, and for Thy holy Israel's sake; to whom Thou hast spoken and promised, that Thou wouldest multiply their seed as the stars of heaven, and as the sand that lies upon the sea shore. For we, O Master, are become less than any nation, and be kept under this day in all the world because of our sins. Neither is there at this time prince, or prophet, or leader, or burnt offering, or sacrifice, or oblation, or incense, or place to sacrifice before Thee, and to find mercy. Nevertheless in a contrite heart and an humble spirit let us be accepted, like as in the burnt offerings of rams and bullocks, and like as in ten thousands of fat lambs: so let our sacrifice be in Thy sight this day, and grant that we may wholly go after Thee: for they shall not be confounded that put their trust in Thee. And now we follow Thee with all our heart, we fear Thee, and seek Thy face; put us not to shame, but deal with us after Thy lovingkindness, and according to the multitude of Thy mercies. Deliver us also according to Thy marvelous works, and give glory to Thy name, O Lord: and let all them that do Thy servants hurt be ashamed; and let them be confounded in all their power and might, and let their strength be broken; and let them know that Thou art God, the only One, and glorious over the whole world. And the king's servants, that put them in, ceased not to make the oven hot with rosin, pitch, tow, and small wood; so that the flame streamed forth above the furnace forty and nine cubits. And it passed through, and burned those Chaldeans it found about the furnace. But the Angel of the Lord came down into the oven together with Azariah and his fellows, and smote the flame of the fire out of the oven; and made the midst of the furnace as it had been a moist whistling wind, so that the fire touched them not at all, neither hurt nor troubled them.[^465] Then the three, as out of one mouth, sang, and blessed, and glorified God in the furnace, saying:
Blessed art Thou, O Lord God of our fathers: to be praised and exalted above all unto ages.  
And blessed is Thy glorious and holy name: to be praised and exalted above all unto ages.  
Blessed art Thou in the temple of Thy holy glory: to be praised and exalted above all unto ages.  
Blessed art Thou that beholdest the depths, and sittest upon the Cherubim: to be praised and exalted above all unto ages.  
Blessed art Thou on the glorious throne of Thy kingdom: to be praised and exalted above all unto ages.  
Blessed art Thou in the firmament of heaven: to be praised and exalted above all unto ages.
*Then the Reader says:*[^466]  
**Reader:** Bless the Lord, all ye works of the Lord; sing:  
*(As indicated above, we sing in Tone 5):*  
Praise Him, and highly exalt Him unto ages!  
**Reader:** O ye Angels of the Lord, and ye heavens of the Lord, bless ye the Lord; sing:  
Praise Him, and highly exalt Him unto ages!  
**Reader:** O all ye waters that be above the heaven, and all ye powers of the Lord, bless ye the Lord; sing:  
Praise Him, and highly exalt Him unto ages!  
**Reader:** O ye sun and moon, and ye stars of heaven, bless ye the Lord; sing:  
Praise Him, and highly exalt Him unto ages!  
**Reader:** O every shower and dew, and all ye spirits, bless ye the Lord; sing:  
Praise Him, and highly exalt Him unto ages!  
**Reader:** O ye fire and heat, winter cold and summer heat, bless ye the Lord; sing:  
Praise Him, and highly exalt Him unto ages!  
**Reader:** O ye dews and hoarfrosts, ice and cold, bless ye the Lord; sing:  
Praise Him, and highly exalt Him unto ages!  
**Reader:** O ye rime and snow, nights and days, bless ye the Lord; sing:  
Praise Him, and highly exalt Him unto ages!  
**Reader:** O ye light and darkness, lightning and clouds, bless ye the Lord; sing:  
Praise Him, and highly exalt Him unto ages!  
**Reader:** O let the earth, the mountains and the hills, and all things that grow on the earth, bless the Lord; sing:  
Praise Him, and highly exalt Him unto ages!  
**Reader:** O ye fountains, seas and rivers, sea monsters and all that move in the waters, bless ye the Lord; sing:  
Praise Him, and highly exalt Him unto ages!  
**Reader:** O all ye fowls of the air, beasts and all cattle, bless ye the Lord; sing:  
Praise Him, and highly exalt Him unto ages!  
**Reader:** O ye children of men, let Israel bless the Lord; sing:  
Praise Him, and highly exalt Him unto ages!  
**Reader:** O ye priests of the Lord, and ye servants of the Lord, bless ye the Lord; sing:  
Praise Him, and highly exalt Him unto ages!  
**Reader:** O ye spirits and souls of the righteous, ye holy and humble men of heart, bless ye the Lord; sing:  
Praise Him, and highly exalt Him unto ages!  
**Reader:** O Ananias, Azarias, and Misael, bless ye the Lord; sing:  
Praise Him, and highly exalt Him unto ages!  
**Reader:** O ye Apostles, Prophets, and Martyrs of the Lord, bless ye the Lord; sing:  
Praise Him, and highly exalt Him unto ages!  
**Reader:** Let us bless the Father, and the Son, and the Holy Spirit, the Lord; let us sing:  
And let us highly exalt Him unto ages!  
**Reader:** Both now and ever, and unto ages of ages. Amen. Sing unto the Lord:  
And highly exalt Him unto ages!  
**Reader:** We praise, we bless, and we worship the Lord, singing:  
And highly exalting Him unto ages!  
*Then the Little Litany. The Priest's ecphonesis:*  
**Priest:** For holy art Thou, O Christ our God...  
*And in place of the Trisagion:*[^467]  
As many as have been baptized into Christ have put on Christ. Alleluia!  
*The Prokeimenon in Tone 8:*  
Let all the earth worship Thee, and sing unto Thee!  
*Verse:* Shout with joy unto the Lord, all the earth; sing praises unto His name!  
*The Epistle to the Romans (pericope 91):*[^468]  
Brethren, as many of us as were baptized into Jesus Christ: *ending with:* in Christ Jesus our Lord.  
**Priest:** Peace be unto thee.  
*Then the Deacon:*  
**Deacon:** Let us attend! Wisdom, let us attend!  
*And immediately the Reader, in place of the Alleluia, says:*[^469]  
**Reader:** Arise, O God, judge the earth!  
*The Clergy sing the same in Tone 7:*  
**Choir:** Arise, O God, judge the earth: for Thou shalt inherit all nations!  
*And the Reader chants these verses:*  
**Reader:** God stands in the congregation of gods; He judges among the gods.  
*Verse 2:* How long will ye judge unjustly, and accept the persons of the wicked?  
*Verse 3:* They know not, neither will they understand; they walk on in darkness.  
*And again the Reader:*  
**Reader:** Arise, O God, judge the earth!  
*And the people sing the same.*

*And the Priest strips off the purple phelonion and vests in white; and reads the Holy Gospel from Matthew (pericope 115): In the end of the Sabbath:*[^470]  
*And the Divine Liturgy of Saint Basil the Great.*  
*In place of the Cherubic Hymn, a work of Saint Basil the Great:*[^471]  
Let all mortal flesh keep silence, and with fear and trembling stand, and ponder nothing earthly within itself: for the King of kings and Lord of lords comes forward to be sacrificed and to be given for food unto the faithful. And the ranks of Angels go before Him with all principality and power: the Many-eyed Cherubim, and the Six-winged Seraphim, covering their eyes and crying aloud the hymn: Alleluia, alleluia, alleluia!  
*Communion Verse:* The Lord awoke as from sleep, and is risen, saving us: Alleluia, *(thrice).*  
*And the Antidoron is given by the Priest, and the Dismissal.*  
*Then, upon the analogion prepared beforehand with bread and wine, the customary blessing of these gifts takes place. The Priest comes forward, censes them, and says the customary prayer of blessing:*[^472]  
**Priest:** O Lord Jesus Christ our God, Who didst bless the five loaves...  
*And having said Amen, we sit down each in his own place, in all silence and reverence. Nor do we go out of the church; but immediately the Cellarer enters and distributes the blessed loaves unto the brethren. But see concerning this below.*  
*Note: But if, on account of some extreme necessity, the Divine Liturgy is not celebrated, we sing everything in the same manner as written above. The Entrance with the Lamp. O Gentle Light:*[^473]  
*The Prokeimenon: The Lord reigns... And the Readings of the day from the Triodion according to their order. After these, the Litany: Let us all say... Then: Vouchsafe, O Lord... After this: Let us complete our evening prayer unto the Lord.*  
*At the Aposticha, Resurrection Stichera, Tone 1:*  
By Thy Passion, O Christ, we have been set free from passions; and by Thy Resurrection we have been delivered from corruption. O Lord, glory to Thee!  
*Verse:* The Lord reigns, He is clothed with majesty!  
Let creation rejoice, let the heavens be glad, let the nations clap their hands with gladness: for Christ our Savior has nailed our sins to the Cross, and having put death to death, has granted us life, raising the fallen Adam of all mankind, as He loves mankind.  
*Verse:* For He has established the world, which shall not be moved.  
O King of heaven and earth, Incomprehensible One, Thou wast willingly crucified for the love of mankind! Hades, having met Thee below, was embittered, and the souls of the righteous, having received Thee, rejoiced; and Adam, seeing Thee the Creator in the nethermost parts, arose. O the marvel! How did the Life of all taste of death? But even as Thou wast pleased to enlighten the world, crying aloud and saying: O Thou Who art risen from the dead, Lord, glory to Thee!  
*Verse 3:* Holiness becomes Thine house, O Lord, unto length of days.  
The Myrrh-bearing women, carrying myrrh, reached Thy Tomb with haste and lamentation; and not finding Thine immaculate Body, but learning of the new and most glorious marvel from the Angel, they said unto the Apostles: The Lord is risen, granting great mercy unto the world!

*Glory, Both now, Theotokion:*  
Behold, the prophecy of Isaiah is fulfilled: for as a Virgin thou didst give birth, and after giving birth thou didst remain even as thou wast before birth. For God was born: therefore nature was newly cleft. But, O Mother of God, despise not the prayers of thy servants offered unto thee in thy temple; but as thou didst hold the Tenderhearted One in thine arms, have compassion upon thy suppliants, and pray that our souls may be saved!  
*And the remainder of the service of Vespers. After the Our Father:*  
*Troparia: The noble Joseph... Glory: When Thou didst descend unto death... Both now: Unto the Myrrh-bearing women... (see fol. 126).[^473] And the Dismissal.*  
*Note: The Ecclesiarch must know with certainty that the dismissal of the Liturgy should take place at the second hour of the night. Even though in the Constitutions of the holy Apostles, and likewise the Fathers command the faithful to prepare themselves until the cockcrow; the holy Sixth Council [Canon 89: to fast until midnight]; while the Studite, Jerusalem, and Holy Mountain Typika command the Saturday service to be completed toward the third hour of the night.*[^474]  
*After the dismissal, we go out from the church into the Refectory, and sit down in our customary manner, each in his own place, in all silence and reverence. The Cellarer enters and distributes unto the brethren a portion of bread, equal to half a pound of bread, evenly apportioned; and six figs, or dates; and one measured cup of wine. But for those who do not have a supply of wine, let the brethren drink beer, or mead.*  
*The Greek Typikon, Triodia, and Octoechoi command the brethren, remaining without leaving the church, to partake of the aforesaid food and drink, while the Reader, immediately after the blessing of the loaves, begins and reads the Acts of the Holy Apostles: concerning this, read below in the Typikon for the blessing of the unleavened bread.*  
*Having risen from the Refectory, we begin Small Compline.*[^475]  
*At which we sing the Canon.*  
*(This Canon is not in the Greek edition, nor does it appoint Compline).*  
*On the Bodily Burial of the Lord God and our Savior Jesus Christ, and on the Lamentation of the Mother of God. A work of Symeon the Logothete. With the Heirmos, to 6, in Tone 6:*  
*Ode 1. Heirmos: He Who with a wave of the sea... (see fol. 138).*  
*Refrain:* Glory to Thee, our God, glory to Thee!  
*Troparion:* Desiring to make alive Thine own creation, my Savior, Thou wast suspended upon the Cross and wounded with nails; while Thy blameless Mother standing by was consumed in heart, O Master, beholding the streams of Thy blood.  
*Troparion:* Having bowed Thy divine neck, my Savior, as God-man Thou didst fall asleep on the Tree; and all creation trembled, and the heavenly ranks of Angels recognized Thee, beholding Thee suffering in the flesh.  
*Glory:*  
*Troparion:* The Mother, desiring secretly to approach Thy secret disciple Joseph, prayed unto him, weeping in secret, that he might ask for Thine immaculate Body from Pilate the unjust judge.  
*Both now:*  
*Theotokion:* Approach Pilate, having unquestioning hope; ask for the immaculate Body of Christ God, and taking It down from the Tree, hide It in thy tomb, lest the pitiless ones again pierce His side!  
*Then the Heirmos, both choirs together.*  
*Ode 3. Heirmos: Thee, Who upon the waters...*  
*Troparion:* None dares to ask for the immaculate Body, hanging visible upon the Tree, breathless, dead, and wounded, and assailed with revilings, and especially pierced in the side.
*Troparion:* Peter the first disciple denied Thee, all the disciples abandoned the Teacher; take pity, O noble one, and having asked, take down the Body of my Master, and do thou alone weep, as for a most cherished child, beloved of a mother!  
*Glory:*  
*Troparion:* When Joseph saw Thy weeping Mother, O my Christ, hearing her painful words, he was moved with compassion, and wept with her, and made haste quickly to ask for Thine immaculate Body.  
*Both now:*  
*Theotokion:* Looking upon Thy commandment, Pilate grants Thy divine Body; and taking It down from the Tree with Nicodemus, Joseph bowed toward the earth, and the earth shook, and Hades surrendered its dead in haste.  
*Ode 4. Heirmos: Foreseeing Thy divine...*  
*Troparion:* Embracing Thee, O all-blameless one, she kissed the wounds of Thine immaculate members, saying: "I will die with Thee, for I cannot bear, O Child, to behold the pouring forth of Thine all-pure blood!"  
*Troparion:* Receiving Thee, she that bore Thee laid Thee upon her knees, O Christ, and weeping, kissed Thy hands, wiping away from Thy side the dripping divine blood, O Master!  
*Glory:*  
*Troparion:* Kissing the speechless mouth, she that bore Thee, O Christ, pressed Thee to herself and weeping, kissed Thee, both man and God!  
*Both now:*  
*Theotokion:* The Persian kings brought Thee gifts, O Master, worshipping Thee as God; but now Nicodemus brings burial ointments, O Child, as for a mortal! How shall I bury Thee, O my Christ?  
*Ode 5. Heirmos: Rising early unto Thee...*  
*Troparion:* "I cannot bear, O Child, to hold Thee breathless, Whom I long held playing as a babe in my arms! If Thou descendest unto Adam into Hades, I also will descend with Thee, to learn Thy mysteries!"  
*Troparion:* "Weep not, O Mother," Christ spoke divinely unto her that bore Him, "for thou shalt see Me risen again, and radiantly proclaiming 'Rejoice!' unto thee, and raising up Adam with Eve!"  
*Glory:*  
*Troparion:* "How shall I endure, O Child, the pains of my womb, kissing Thy face which gazed from the Tree, which formerly I saw shining with beautiful comeliness, and enlightening the blind by Thy divine behest?"  
*Both now:*  
*Theotokion:* "Shall I rather go down to see Thy glory in Hades, O my Son, and ascend again? For I cannot bear to hold Thy lifeless body motionless, and to kiss Thy wounded members!"  
*Ode 6. Heirmos: Held fast, but not consumed...*  
*Troparion:* "How hast thou denied the Savior, O Peter, Whom thou didst confess as Christ, the Son of the living God, and hast left Him alone upon the Tree, crucified, without breath and without form; neither hast thou made haste quickly to hide His Body in a tomb?" the pure Mother wept and spoke.  
*Troparion:* "Give me a word, O sweetest Word and Teacher!" the pure one cried unto Thee with weeping; "I cannot bear to lay Thee in the tomb without breath, O my Son, Who art free among the dead! Behold how Thine own Mother weeps without ceasing; for a burning sword has pierced my heart!"  
*Glory:*

*Troparion:* "Come, all ye Heavenly Powers, behold the Body of Christ abandoned by friends and acquaintances, and His chosen disciple, and be ye struck with awe! I alone, weeping and lamenting my slaughtered Child, with the one beloved disciple His apostle!"  
*Both now:*  
*Theotokion:* The companion Myrrh-bearing woman called out, O Word, undefiled and pure, and joined in her lamentation: "Come, companion women, weep with crying out for the much-suffering Body of the Teacher! Come, behold the speechless mouth, motionless to the disciples, the closed eyes of Him in Whose hand is the breath of the living!"  
*Kontakion, Tone 6:*[^476]  
He Who shut up the abyss is seen dead; wrapped with myrrh and a winding-sheet, the Immortal One is laid in a tomb as a mortal; and women came to anoint Him with myrrh, weeping bitterly and crying out: "This is the most blessed Sabbath, in which Christ, having fallen asleep, shall rise again on the third day!"  
*Ikos:*  
He Who holds all things was lifted up upon the Cross, and all creation laments, beholding Him hanging naked upon the Tree; the sun withdrew its rays, and the stars set their light; the earth with great fear was shaken, and the sea fled, and the rocks were cleft; many tombs were opened, and the bodies of the saints arose, shining radiantly; Hades groans below; the Jews take counsel to slander the Resurrection of Christ; while the women cry out: "This is the most blessed Sabbath, in which Christ, having fallen asleep, shall rise again on the third day!"  
*Ode 7. Heirmos: Ineffable wonder:*  
The angelic hosts, looking down from heaven, were astonished, seeing Him dead upon His Mother's bosom, Whom the Cherubim behold with trembling upon the throne, Who is in the bosom of the Father and has raised the dead from the tombs.  
*Troparion:*  
The ranks of Angels, seeing the Body of Christ lying breathless upon His Mother's bosom, and seized with wonder, turned back with fear, and awestruck cried out: "Blessed art Thou, O God our Deliverer!"  
*Glory:*  
*Troparion:*  
The Virgin, speaking of the mystery of Thy birth, O Master, wept bitterly: "Woe is me, O Gabriel! Where are the glad tidings? Where is to me the 'Rejoice'? Where is the 'Blessed art thou'? For the Light from my womb has been quenched upon the Cross!"  
*Both now:*  
*Theotokion:*  
"How have the lawless people not spared Thee, O sweetest Child, seeing Thee hanging naked upon the Tree, but like wild beasts have pierced Thy divine side? How has creation not fallen apart in trembling? But all things together are held fast by Thy hand, O Word!"  
*Ode 8. Heirmos: Be astonished, O heaven, in fear:*  
Be astonished, O heaven and earth, and hide, O sun, the rays of thy light! For behold, the Light has been placed in darkness, God Who shone forth from my womb as the only Light has set upon the Tree, desiring to resurrect Adam; and holding Him breathless, I cannot bear the pain!  
*Troparion:*  
"How shall I cover Thy most pure Body in a dark tomb, O my Son and God, Who didst empty the tombs? And I shrink back with my hands, not looking upon Thee, O Child; I cannot bear to depart from Thee, O my Son, but rather will I die and go down into Hades!"  
*Glory:*  
*Troparion:*  
"Hear, O heaven and earth, and give ear to my maternal lamentations! For behold, your Judge has been crucified by lawless hands, and lo, He appears without form and without breath upon His Mother's bosom, slain by those who crucified Him, the Creator!"
*Both now:*  
*Theotokion:*  
When the pure one cried out: "Would that I might hear the sweetest speech of Thy voice, and that His lips might move becomingly, and Thine eyes look radiantly, beautiful in comeliness, so that, O my Son, I might see Thee risen again, and be filled with joy instead of bitter tears!"  
*Ode 9. Heirmos: Weep not for Me, O Mother:*  
"Instead of swaddling bands, O my Son, I have wrapped Thee in a shroud; and instead of a manger, O my Light, I lay Thee in a dark tomb; and instead of milk, I sprinkle Thy most pure lips with tears; and instead of living and beautiful words, Thou art dead without speech!"  
*Troparion:*  
"Weep not for Me, O Mother, seeing as mortal in a tomb Him Whom in swaddling bands thou didst behold as God, reverently worshipped by the Persian Magi, and when the Angels truly cried out: 'Glory to Him Who is risen!' For unto thee first will I appear!"  
*Glory:*  
*Troparion:*  
"Go, O Child, into Hades, as God to resurrect with Adam those who through Adam fell into corruption; while I, shedding maternal tears before the gates right opposite Thy tomb, will sit, O my God, until Thou shalt rise again!"  
*Both now:*  
*Theotokion:*  
"O all-blessed Mother, weep not tears henceforth before the gates of the tomb; for I will arise and be glorified, and will radiantly proclaim unto thee 'Rejoice!', and having ascended into heaven, I will send unutterable joy!"  
*Then instead of "It is truly meet", the Heirmos: Weep not for Me, O Mother.*  
*After this, the Trisagion; after the Our Father, Kontakion: He Who shut up the abyss... And the rest as customary, and the Dismissal.*  
*Chapter 98:*[^477]  
*It is necessary to know that when the Paschal Key is ("Г"), then on March 25 the Annunciation of the Most Holy Theotokos coincides with the Holy and Great Sunday of Pascha itself, which is called Kyriopascha, that is, the Lord's Pascha jointly celebrated with the Feast of the Annunciation.*  
*At Vespers on Great Saturday, at "Lord, I have cried":*  
*Resurrection Stichera, 3; and of Great Saturday, 3; and of the Feast, 4. Glory, Tone 6: Today secretly... Both now, of the Feast, in the same Tone: The Archangel Gabriel was sent... Entrance with the Gospel. "O Gladsome Light." The Prokeimenon is not sung, but immediately the Priest: "Wisdom!" And the first reading of the day is read: "In the beginning God made heaven and earth" (one Paremia). Then the 5 readings of the Feast of the Annunciation, and the remaining readings of the day in their order. And in order, the Divine Liturgy of Saint Basil the Great.*  
*After the dismissal of the Liturgy, there is waiting and reading of the Acts of the Holy Apostles according to their order, as indicated in the present Chapter for Great Saturday on folio 163 (see fol. 163)[^477], and below on folio 164.*  
*And when the reading is finished, the Priest blesses, and we say: The Trisagion; and after the Our Father: Lord, have mercy, 12 times. Glory, and now: Come, let us worship, thrice. Then Psalm 50. And we sing the Canon of the Feast with the Heirmos, to 6; and of Great Saturday, the Canon with the Heirmos, to 8. Katavasia: the Heirmos of Saturday, both choirs together. After Ode 3, the Hypakoë and Ikos of the Feast; then the Sessional Hymn, Glory, and now, of the Feast, and the reading of the Feast: Again, the Joy of the Annunciation. After Ode 6, the Kontakion and Ikos of the day, and the reading of the day. At Ode 8 we do not sing "Thou hast been quenched". After Ode 9, the Trisagion; and after the Our Father, Troparion: When Thou didst descend unto death... Glory, and now, of the Feast: Today is the fountainhead of our salvation... Ektenia and Dismissal.*  
*If the time is early, the reading of the day is added. While it is being read, the Ecclesiarch distributes candles to the brethren. Thereupon the lighter, having taken a blessing from the Superior, goes out and strikes all the bells and clappers.*

*And after the reading we all go out into the Narthex, the Superior walking before with a lamp. And when the doors are closed, the Superior censes all the brethren according to order. Then standing before the great church doors, making the sign of the Cross thrice with the censer, he proclaims:*  
*Glory to the Holy and Consubstantial... as in order. And when we have answered: Amen, the Superior begins the Troparion, Tone 5: Christ is risen from the dead... thrice. And after this the Clerics sing the same verse thrice. Then the Superior says Verse 1: Let God arise... Clerics: Christ is risen... thrice. The Superior again, Verse 2: As smoke vanishes... Clerics: Christ is risen... thrice. The Superior, Verse 3: So let the sinners perish... Clerics: Christ is risen... thrice. The Superior, Verse 4: This is the day which the Lord has made... Clerics: Christ is risen... Then the Superior himself sings with a loud voice: Christ is risen... halfway. And when the doors are opened, he enters into the church, the candle-bearers going before, the Superior and Clerics singing together: And to those in the graves He has granted life. And when the Superior has entered into the Sanctuary, he says: Glory; and we sing the Troparion of the Annunciation, Tone 4: Today is the fountainhead of our salvation... Both now: the same. Then the Great Ektenia: In peace let us pray to the Lord. And after the exclamation, the Superior begins the Heirmos: The day of Resurrection! And we sing the Canon of Pascha with the Heirmos, to 8; and the Canon of the Annunciation with the Heirmos, to 8. Afterward the Katavasia: the Heirmos of both Canons, each choir its own. And after each Ode, the Little Ektenia and exclamations. After Ode 3, the Kontakion and Ikos of Pascha, and the Ypakoë. And reading in Gregory the Theologian, of which the beginning is: "I will stand upon my watch." After Ode 6, the Kontakion and Ikos of the Annunciation, and the reading from the same Theologian, of which the beginning is: "The day of Resurrection." Then the Festal Prokeimenon, Tone 4: Proclaim the good tidings from day to day... Verse: Sing unto the Lord a new song... "Let every breath praise the Lord." The Gospel from Luke (Chapter 4): "In those days, Mary arose..." Then: Having beheld the Resurrection of Christ... thrice. Psalm 50 is not said. Then the Sticheron: Jesus is risen, thrice, in Tone 6. Then Ode 7 and the remaining two. At Ode 9 we do not sing "More honorable than the Cherubim". Exapostilarion of Pascha, twice; and of the Feast, twice. At the Praises, Resurrection Stichera, to 4; and of the Annunciation, to 4, with their verses: Proclaim the good tidings... Verse 2: Sing unto the Lord... Then both choirs together, Verse 1: Let God arise, and let His enemies be scattered... Stichera of Pascha, Tone 5, with the remaining verses. Glory, of the Annunciation, Tone 2: Let the heavens rejoice... Both now, of Pascha, Tone 5: The day of Resurrection, let us be radiant, O people! And the customary Kiss of Peace. And the reading of Chrysostom. Then the Ektenia and Dismissal.*  
*There is no procession outside the Monastery, nor is the Catechetical Homily sung after Pascha.*  
*AT THE LITURGY:*  
*Antiphons of Pascha. After the Entrance we say: In the assemblies bless ye God, the Lord from the fountains of Israel. And: Christ is risen... thrice. Glory: Troparion: Today is the fountainhead of our salvation... Both now: Kontakion of Pascha: Though Thou didst descend into the grave... And instead of the Trisagion we sing: As many of you as have been baptized into Christ... The Prokeimenon, Epistle, Alleluia, and Gospel first of Pascha, then of the Feast. And in order, the Divine Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom. Communion Verse of Pascha: Receive the Body of Christ... and another of the Feast: For the Lord has chosen Zion, He has chosen it for a habitation unto Himself.*  
*Note: If the Apodosis of the Annunciation of the Most Holy Theotokos occurs on Palm Sunday, or on Pascha, or on any day of Bright Week, then at the Liturgy the Epistle and Gospel of the Annunciation are omitted.*  
*If the Apodosis occurs on any day, we sing the Archangel Gabriel and the Service of the Most Pure Theotokos together at Compline, except in their own dedicated temples.*  
*If the Commemoration of the Holy Great-Martyr George occurs on Holy Pascha, concerning this see the Rubric on folio 151 (see fol. 151)[^477], Mark Chapter 103.*

*After the dismissal of Compline, if there be one, or as said above on folio 165:*  
*There is the Reading in the Acts of the Holy Apostles, from the beginning.[^478] The Reader says the beginning, and the Priest the verse:*  
**Priest:** Through the prayers of the Holy Apostles, O Lord Jesus Christ our God, have mercy on us.  
*And the Reader begins to read.*  
*All the brethren listening with diligence, let it not be permitted for anyone to give himself unto sleep, fearing the defilement of the adversary the tempter. For at such times and in such places the enemy strives to defile careless and slumberous monks. And the Readers relieve one another. With such vigilance, as in all church offices, the head of the church must take heed. And they read until the fourth hour of the night, or as far as they reach. And all the Acts are read to the end.*  
*After this, the Paraecclesiarch lights all the lamps in their places.[^479] And having taken a blessing from the Superior, he goes out and strikes the wooden clapper. The Reader concludes the reading. And immediately, the Priest having given the blessing, we say the Trisagion; and after the Our Father: Lord, have mercy, 12 times. Glory, and now: Come, let us worship, thrice. Then Psalm 50. And the Canon of Great Saturday Matins, Tone 6: "He Who with a wave of the sea..." The Heirmos twice, and the Troparia to 12. And after Ode 9, the Heirmos, both choirs together. After Ode 3, the Sessional Hymn: The soldiers guarding Thy tomb, O Savior... Glory, and now, the same.*  
*And the Homily of Saint Epiphanios of Cyprus is read, of which the beginning is: "What is this? Today there is a great silence upon the earth..."*[^480]  
*After Ode 6, the Kontakion and Ikos of Great Saturday.*  
*Then the reading from the Homilies of Chrysostom on Matthew at the end of the Gospel commentary, or the universal Homily on John. After this, Ode 7.*  
*After Ode 9, we sing one Heirmos, both choirs together, and a prostration to the earth.*  
*And after the conclusion of the Canon, the Trisagion; and after the Our Father, the Troparion: When Thou didst descend unto death, O Life Immortal... Then the Ektenia: Have mercy on us, O God, according to Thy great mercy, we pray Thee, hear and have mercy.*  
*And the Dismissal.*  
*If the head of the church wishes, the reading is not appointed here.*  
*After this, the Ecclesiarch distributes candles to the brethren. The Paraecclesiarch, having taken a blessing from the Superior, goes out and strikes the great bell, and tolls the clappers sufficiently. And having entered the church, he lights all the candles and church lamps before the Holy Icons, and the polykandela in the midst of the church; and he prepares burning coals in two vessels abundantly, and they place in them fragrant incense sufficiently, so that the entire church is filled with fragrance. One vessel is placed in the midst of the church opposite the Holy Doors, and the other is set before the Holy Doors. And inside the holy sanctuary, the lamp burns with incense.*  
*Then the Superior, having entered into the holy sanctuary with the priests and deacons, they vest in all their most radiant vestments. And the Superior, having taken in his hands the censer and the Precious Cross, and the other priests and deacons take the Holy Gospel and the holy precious Cross, the Resurrection, and the icons of the Most Pure Theotokos, and the other holy and precious icons according to their order. And all the brethren light their candles, going out into the narthex.*
*And as the Superior goes out from the church with the priests into the Narthex, all the candle-bearers precede him, with the deacons and two with candles; and both choirs sing the Sticheron, Tone 6:*  
Thy Resurrection, O Christ Savior, the Angels sing in the heavens; and make us worthy on earth to praise and glorify Thee with a pure heart!  
*And having entered into the Narthex, they lock the church doors which face the west. And the Superior censes all the brethren according to custom in the Narthex, as is fitting, a deacon preceding him with a burning lamp. And all the brethren stand, if there be room, likewise in the Narthex or before the church, holding their candles, praying within themselves with attention, and thanking Christ our God Who suffered and rose again for our sake.*  
*And at the conclusion of the censing, the Superior comes before the great church doors and censes the deacon standing before him with the lamp. And then the deacon, taking the censer from the hands of the Superior, censes him also. And the Superior, having taken the censer again, stands before the church doors looking toward the east, and signs the closed great doors of the church with the holy censer in the form of a cross thrice, holding in his hand the Precious Cross, the candle-bearers standing on either side or held by clerics; and with a loud voice he proclaims:*[^481]

---

### Notes

[^3]: Kathisma 18 of the Psalter comprises Psalms 119–133 (LXX) [Masoretic 120–134], the Psalms of Ascents (Gradual Psalms), traditionally appointed for weekday Vespers during Great Lent.
[^4]: Psalm 141:8 (LXX) [Masoretic 142:7]: 'Bring my soul out of prison, that I may give thanks unto Thy name.'
[^5]: The venerable Lenten dismissal sticheron 'Душеполезную совершивше Четыредесятницу...' (Having completed the forty days that bring profit to our souls) marks the statutory conclusion of the Holy Forty Days of Great Lent and the opening of Great and Holy Passion Week.
[^6]: Leo VI the Wise (866–912), Byzantine Emperor and hymnographer, author of the Byzantine Idiomela for Lazarus Saturday and the eleven Sunday Evangelical Stichera (Eothina).
[^7]: Psalm 123:8 (LXX) [Masoretic 124:8].
[^8]: Genesis 49:29–50:26 (LXX), the concluding narrative of the Book of Genesis detailing the death and burial of the Patriarch Jacob and the death of Joseph in Egypt.
[^9]: Psalm 124:1–3 (LXX) [Masoretic 125:1–3].
[^10]: Proverbs 31:8–31 (LXX), King Lemuel's praise of the virtuous and valiant woman ('Жену мужественну кто обрящетъ'), read on Lazarus Friday as a prophetic type of the Church.
[^11]: Psalm 33 (LXX) [Masoretic 34], traditionally chanted at the conclusion of the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts and at the Typika.
[^12]: Psalm 103 (LXX) [Masoretic 104], the Prooemiac (Introductory) Psalm of Vespers.
[^13]: Psalm 122:1 (LXX) [Masoretic 123:1].
[^14]: The Troparion of Lazarus Saturday, Tone 1: 'Giving us before Thy Passion an assurance of the general resurrection, Thou didst raise Lazarus from the dead, O Christ God...'.
[^15]: Markian rubric governing the complete suspension of the Octoechos, common weekday Theotokia, and common Martyrika from Lazarus Saturday through the entire duration of Great and Holy Passion Week and Bright Week, resuming only on the Saturday of Saint Thomas (Antipascha).
[^16]: Detailed Markian chapter specifying the concurrence (kyriopascha-type rubrical confluence) when the Great Feast of the Annunciation of the Most Holy Theotokos (March 25) coincides with Lazarus Saturday.
[^17]: Psalm 95:2 (LXX) [Masoretic 96:2], festal prokeimenon stichos of the Annunciation.
[^18]: Psalm 95:1 (LXX) [Masoretic 96:1].
[^19]: Psalm 50 (LXX) [Masoretic 51], the Penitential Psalm recited at daily and festal Matins.
[^20]: Psalm 9:33 (LXX 9:32) [Masoretic 10:12].
[^21]: Psalm 9:2 (LXX 9:1).
[^22]: Galatians 3:27: 'As many of you as have been baptized into Christ have put on Christ. Alleluia.' In the ancient Byzantine and Rus' liturgical traditions, Lazarus Saturday was a primary baptismal day of the catechumens, for which reason Galatians 3:27 permanently replaces the Trisagion at the Divine Liturgy.
[^23]: Saint Andrew of Crete (ca. 660–740), Archbishop of Gortyna and author of the Great Penitential Canon, who also composed the Triodion canons for Lazarus Saturday and Holy Week.
[^24]: Deuteronomy 32:1 (LXX), the Biblical Canticle of Moses ('Give ear, O heaven, and I will speak...'), which forms the scriptural basis for Ode 2 of Byzantine liturgical canons.
[^25]: Psalm 117:22 (LXX) [Masoretic 118:22]; quoted also in Matthew 21:42, Mark 12:10, and Luke 20:17.
[^26]: Habakkuk 3:11 (LXX): 'The sun was lifted up, and the moon stood in its order; into light Thy weapons shall go forth, unto the shine of the glittering of Thine arms.' Saint Andrew of Crete interprets the celestial standing-still as a prophetic type of the sun darkening and Christ being lifted up upon the Tree of the Cross.
[^27]: 1 Kings 17:17–24 (LXX 3 Kings 17:17–24) and 2 Kings 4:32–37 (LXX 4 Kings 4:32–37). Saint Andrew emphasizes the qualitative pre-eminence of Christ's miracle over the Old Testament resurrections: Elijah raised the son of the widow of Zarephath, and Elisha raised the son of the Shunammite woman, but in both instances the bodies were newly deceased in the domestic bed-chamber, whereas Christ raised Lazarus after four days from within a sealed sepulcher when bodily corruption had already commenced.
[^28]: Isaiah 26:12–13 (LXX): 'O Lord our God, grant us peace, for Thou hast rendered all things unto us. O Lord our God, take possession of us; O Lord, besides Thee we know no other, we name Thy name.' This Biblical Canticle serves as the scriptural ode-theme for Ode 5 of the Byzantine canon.
[^29]: Jonah 2:4–6 (LXX): 'Thou didst cast me into the depths of the heart of the sea... and Thou didst bring up my life from corruption, O Lord my God.'
[^30]: Allusion to the prophetic vision of the valley of dry bones in Ezekiel 37:4–8 (LXX), wherein the Lord knits the bones together with sinews, brings flesh upon them, and breathes into them the breath of life; here poetically placed on the lips of Lazarus crying aloud from within Hades.
[^31]: Daniel 3:49–52 (LXX), the Biblical Canticle of the Three Holy Children in the Babylonian furnace (Ananias, Azarias, and Misael), providing the foundational biblical canticle for Ode 7 of Byzantine liturgical canons.
[^32]: A hallmark of Byzantine patristic dramatic hymnography (developed richly in the kontakia of Saint Romanos the Melodist and the canons of Saint Andrew of Crete), wherein personified Hades experiences severe digestive recoil upon swallowing Lazarus, viewing him as an emetic substance forced upon him by Christ that threatens to compel the evacuation of all held captive from Adam onward.
[^33]: Daniel 3:59–60 (LXX) / The Prayer of Azarias and the Song of the Three Holy Youths (Benedicite): 'O ye heavens, bless ye the Lord; O all ye waters that be above the heaven, bless ye the Lord; praise and exalt Him unto ages of ages.' Scriptural basis for Ode 8.
[^34]: The Biblical Canticles of the New Testament: the Song of the Theotokos (Magnificat, Luke 1:51–52) and the Song of Zacharias (Benedictus, Luke 1:78–79 LXX): 'Through the tender mercy of our God, whereby the Dayspring from on high hath visited us, to give light to them that sit in darkness and in the shadow of death, to guide our feet into the way of peace.'
[^35]: Luke 7:11–15: Christ's raising of the son of the widow of Nain as he was being carried out upon the funeral bier.
[^36]: In the Byzantine and Ruthenian typikon, the Midnight Office on Lazarus Saturday undergoes notable festive modifications marking the end of the Great Fast: the customary penitential troparia and the prayer of Saint Eustratius ('Remember, O Lord, our fathers and brethren who have fallen asleep...') are omitted, replaced by the Troparion of the feast (*By raising Lazarus from the dead...*) and the festive Kontakion.
[^37]: The Troparion of Lazarus Saturday (Tone 1, *By raising Lazarus from the dead...*), which is retained identically as the primary festal dismissal troparion on Palm Sunday, inextricably linking the raising of Lazarus with Christ's triumphal entry into Jerusalem.
[^38]: Psalm 109:1 (LXX 109:1) [Masoretic 110:1]: 'The Lord said unto my Lord: Sit Thou at My right hand, until I make Thine enemies the footstool of Thy feet.' This begins Kathisma 16 of the Psalter, chanted at Saturday Matins.
[^39]: Melodic model (*Podoben*): *Tone 1, Podoben: "While the stone was sealed" (Ка́мени зна́менану)*. A primary automelon of Tone 1 drawn from the Sunday Resurrectional Troparion of that tone.
[^40]: Psalm 118:12 (LXX) with the Resurrectional Evlogitaria (*Благословенъ еси, Господи, научи мя оправданіемъ твоимъ. Ангельскій соборъ удивися...*). Lazarus Saturday is the only non-Sunday in the entire Byzantine liturgical year on which the full Sunday Resurrectional Evlogitaria and the hymn *Having beheld the Resurrection of Christ* (*Воскресеніе Христово видѣвше*) are chanted at Matins, emphasizing its status as the solemn annual fore-feast of the Lord's Resurrection.
[^41]: Melodic model (*Podoben*): *Tone 5, Podoben: "Co-beginning with the Father" (Събезнача́лное сло́во)*. Drawn from the Sunday Resurrectional Troparion of Tone 5 (*Let us, the faithful, praise and worship the Word, co-unoriginate with the Father and the Spirit...*).
[^42]: Saint John Chrysostom, *Homilies on the Gospel of Saint John*, Homilies 63 and 64 (PG 59:349–358), commenting on John 11:1–44 (the illness, death, and raising of Lazarus). In the classic monastic typika (including the Studite and Sabaite recensions followed in Ruthenia), these homilies were appointed to be read aloud from the *Prologue* or *Margareton* during the kathisma intervals of Matins.
[^43]: Saint Theophanes the Branded (*Graptos*, ca. 778–845), Metropolitan of Nicaea and renowned iconodule confessor, author of numerous Triodion and Pentecostarion canons. The biblical canticle theme of Ode 1 is drawn from Exodus 15:1 (LXX).
[^44]: Saint John the Monk (Saint John Damascene, ca. 676–749), author of the Octoechos and the Paschal Canon. His second canon for Lazarus Saturday Matins is chanted to the famous heirmologic model *Crossing the water as on dry land* (*Во́дꙋ проше́дъ ꙗ҆́кѡ сꙋхꙋ̀*).
[^45]: Ode 3 Katavasia: *Tone 8, Heirmos: "O Lord, the Architect of the vault of heaven" (Небе́сному кру́гу верхотво́рче Го́споди)*, a classical heirmos attributed to Saint John of Damascus.
[^46]: Melodic model (*Podoben*): *Tone 4, Podoben: "Joseph marveled" (Оу҆диви́ся І҆ѡ́сифъ)*. A primary automelon of Tone 4 celebrating the mysteries of Christ's incarnation and resurrection.
[^47]: Melodic model (*Podoben*): *Tone 8, Podoben: "Of Wisdom" / "That which was mystically commanded" (Премꙋ́дрости)*.
[^48]: Habakkuk 3:2 (LXX): 'O Lord, I have heard Thy report, and was afraid; O Lord, I considered Thy works, and was astonished.'
[^49]: Second Canon Ode 4 Heirmos: *Tone 8, Heirmos: "Thou art my strength, O Lord" (Ты моѧ̀ крѣ́пость Го́споди)*, based on the Prayer of Habakkuk (Habakkuk 3:19 LXX).
[^50]: Psalm 42:2 (LXX) [Masoretic 43:2] and Psalm 87:15 (LXX) [Masoretic 88:14]: 'Therefore hast Thou cast me off? Why go I mourning while the enemy oppresses me?' Combined with Isaiah 26:9.
[^51]: Saint Cosmas the Melodist (Bishop of Maiuma, ca. 706–760). In Byzantine hymnography, from Ode 6 through Ode 9, Saint Cosmas provided four-ode sets (Tetraodia) that amplify the celebratory climax of the matins canon on Great and Holy Week and Lazarus Saturday.
[^52]: Jonah 2:1–3 (LXX): Jonah's prayer from the belly of the sea-monster (*kētos*), the universal Old Testament type of Christ's three-day burial and resurrection.
[^53]: Melodic model (*Podoben*): *Tone 2, Podoben: "Seeking the highest" (Въ вы́шнихъ и҆щѧ̀)*, composed by Saint Romanos the Melodist.
[^54]: The Ikos of Saint Romanos the Melodist for Lazarus Saturday, preserving the early colometric dialogue between Christ and the Apostles prior to their departure from Perea unto Bethany.
[^55]: The introductory iambic verses (*stichoi*) of the Synaxarion, composed in Byzantine Greek by Nikephoros Kallistos Xanthopoulos (ca. 1256–1335) for the liturgical services of the Triodion and Pentecostarion.
[^56]: An ancient Palestinian and Byzantine exegetical tradition (recorded also in the Synaxarion of Constantinople) identifying Simon the Pharisee (in whose house the sinful woman anointed Christ, Luke 7:36–50) or Simon the Leper (Matthew 26:6) as the father of Lazarus, Martha, and Mary.
[^57]: According to early Church tradition, Lazarus was forced to flee Judea due to the homicidal plots of the chief priests (John 12:10–11). He arrived in Cyprus, where the Holy Apostles Paul and Barnabas ordained him as the first Bishop of Kition (modern-day Larnaca).
[^58]: Cypriot and Athonite sacred traditions concerning the second life of Saint Lazarus: having beheld the terrors of Hades during his four days of death, he remained profoundly grave, never laughing or smiling throughout the remainder of his life (save once when observing someone steal a clay vessel, remarking: 'Earth steals earth'); he partook only of sweets to alleviate the lingering bitterness of the tomb; and during her visit to Cyprus, the Most Holy Theotokos personally presented him with an episcopal omophorion and epigonation woven by her own hands.
[^59]: The translation of the holy relics of Saint Lazarus from Kition in Cyprus to Constantinople took place in the year 890 under Byzantine Emperor Leo VI the Wise (886–912), who built a splendid basilica dedicated to the saint in the imperial capital and composed festive stichera for his feast.
[^60]: The patristic rationale (synthesized by Nikephoros Kallistos) explaining why the Synoptic Evangelists (Matthew, Mark, and Luke) did not record the raising of Lazarus: when their Gospels were compiled and circulated, Lazarus was still alive in Cyprus, and publishing the miracle would have endangered his life by renewing the persecuting fury of the Jewish authorities; whereas Saint John wrote late in the first century, long after Lazarus had reposed in the Lord.
[^61]: An ancient Byzantine and Ruthenian linguistic and rubrical custom: burial winding-sheets and shrouds were known as *lazaroma* (Greek: λάζαρωма; Slavonic: ла́зарома), and any departed Christian was mystically designated a 'Lazarus', resting in anticipation of hearing the voice of Christ and the final trumpet at the general resurrection (1 Corinthians 15:52; 1 Thessalonians 4:16).
[^62]: Daniel 3:26–27 (LXX): The prayer and thanksgiving of Azarias and the Hebrew children in the furnace of Babylon.
[^63]: Second Canon Ode 7 Heirmos: *Tone 8, Heirmos: "The Youths that came from Judea" (Ѿ І҆оуде́а доше́дшїи)*, another classic heirmos composed by Saint John Damascene.
[^64]: Daniel 3:5–7, 18 (LXX): The refusal of the Three Youths to bow down before Nebuchadnezzar's golden image on the plain of Dura, despite the sound of the sackbut, psaltery, symphony, and all musical instruments.
[^65]: Second Canon Ode 8 Heirmos: *Tone 8, Heirmos: "The King of Heaven, Whom the hosts of angels praise" (Царя́ Небе́снаго, Е҆го́же пою́тъ во́и а҆́нгельстїи)*.
[^66]: Liturgical rubric: *Честнѣйшую не поемъ* ('More honorable than the Cherubim is not sung'). On Lazarus Saturday, the Canticle of the Theotokos (the Magnificat) is omitted at Ode 9, and the ninth ode of the feast is sung immediately with its festal heirmoi and troparia, an honor reserved for Dominical and major feasts in the Byzantine and Ruthenian rite.
[^67]: The Biblical Canticle of the Mother of God and Zacharias (Luke 1:46–55, 68–79 LXX); the ninth ode of the Byzantine canon. Saint Theophanes the Branded juxtaposes the miraculous childbearing of the Theotokos—who held the divine fire of the Godhead within her womb without being consumed, like the unburnt bush on Sinai (Exodus 3:2)—with Christ's divine power raising Lazarus from the corruption of the four-day tomb.
[^68]: Exaposteilarion melodic model: *Tone 3, Podoben: "He hath visited us" (Посѣти́лъ ны є҆́стъ)*. The incipit derives from Luke 1:78 (LXX): 'The Dayspring from on high hath visited us.'
[^69]: Melodic model (*Automelon*): *Tone 1, Idiomelon / Samoglasen*. The Praises (Ainoi) on Lazarus Saturday open with Psalm 149:9 (LXX): 'This glory shall be to all His saints.' The four appointed stichera systematically articulate the orthodox Christology defined at the Council of Chalcedon (451 AD): Christ possesses two distinct and unconfused natures, human and divine, operating in complete synergy. He weeps and inquires concerning the location of the tomb as true man, yet raises the decomposed corpse after four days by His sovereign divine will as true God.
[^70]: Psalm 150:1 (LXX): 'Praise God in His saints; praise Him in the firmament of His power.'
[^71]: Psalm 150:2 (LXX): 'Praise Him for His mighty acts; praise Him according to the multitude of His greatness.'
[^72]: Psalm 150:3 (LXX): 'Praise Him with the sound of the trumpet; praise Him with the psaltery and harp.'
[^73]: Psalm 150:4 (LXX): 'Praise Him with timbrel and choir; praise Him with strings and the organ.'
[^74]: Psalm 150:5–6 (LXX): 'Praise Him with high-sounding cymbals; praise Him with cymbals of jubilation. Let every breath praise the Lord.'
[^75]: Psalm 9:33 (LXX 9:33) [Masoretic 10:12]: 'Arise, O Lord my God, let Thy hand be lifted up; forget not Thy poor unto the end.'
[^76]: Psalm 9:2 (LXX 9:2): 'I will give thanks unto Thee, O Lord, with my whole heart; I will speak of all Thy marvelous works.'
[^77]: Galatians 3:27: At the Divine Liturgy on Lazarus Saturday, the customary Trisagion (*Holy God, Holy Mighty, Holy Immortal*) is replaced by the baptismal troparion *As many as have been baptized into Christ have put on Christ. Alleluia*. In early Christian and Byzantine practice, Lazarus Saturday was one of the solemn annual days for the baptism of catechumens, preparing them to enter into Holy Week and celebrate Bright Pascha.
[^78]: Hebrews 12:28–13:8 (Pericope 333): 'Therefore, receiving a kingdom which cannot be moved, let us have grace... Jesus Christ the same yesterday, and today, and unto ages of ages.'
[^79]: John 11:1–45 (Pericope 39): The Holy Gospel reading recounting the sickness, death, mourning, and four-day resurrection of Saint Lazarus in Bethany by Jesus Christ.
[^80]: Psalm 8:3 (LXX 8:3) [Masoretic 8:2]: 'Out of the mouths of babes and sucklings hast Thou perfected praise, because of Thine enemies, that Thou mightest destroy the enemy and the avenger.' This verse serves as the Communion Hymn of Lazarus Saturday and the prokeimenon throughout Palm Sunday.
[^81]: Ancient Byzantine and Ruthenian monastic dietary rule (*Trapeza*) for Lazarus Saturday: although falling within the Great Fast, the strict fast is relaxed in honor of the raising of Lazarus to allow cooked food with oil, wine, and fish roe (caviar / *ikra*), three ounces (*ongii*) being apportioned to each monastic brother, whereas whole fish is reserved for Palm Sunday.
[^82]: A typikon instruction (*Zri*) for parishes or monasteries whose temple is dedicated to Saint Lazarus (*Khram Pravednaho Lazara*). In such temples, the patronal service is elevated to a full vigil with Polyeleos, Megalynarion, the reading of the Life of Saint Lazarus written by Nikephoros Xanthopoulos, Anabathmoi of Tone 4, Gospel reading from John (Pericope 36), and festive litany stichera.
[^83]: Melodic model (*Podoben*): *Tone 6, Podoben: "Having risen on the third day" (Тре́тїй дне́сь въскр҃лъ є҆сѝ Хрⷭ҇тѐ)*. An automelon of Tone 6 commemorating Christ's third-day resurrection.
[^84]: Greek: *baïa* (βάϊα), Slavonic: *baïa* (ба́їа). The technical liturgical loanword specifically designating palm branches (from Phoenix dactylifera), carried in procession alongside willow branches (*verba* / *vѣtvïe*) in Slavic and Ruthenian lands.
[^85]: Melodic model (*Podoben*): *Tone 2, Podoben: "O House of Ephratha" (До́ме Є҆ѵфра́ѳовъ)*. A classic automelon of Tone 2, originally composed for the Forefeast of the Nativity of Christ, here applied to the entrance of Christ into Jerusalem.
[^86]: The Dismissal Troparion of Lazarus Saturday (Tone 1, *By raising Lazarus from the dead...* / *Ѡ҆́бщее въскр҃се́нїе*), which is retained on Palm Sunday as the primary festive dismissal troparion.
[^87]: Folio 10 (leaf p14), where the Troparion of Lazarus Saturday was printed in full at Small Vespers.
[^88]: The second festal Dismissal Troparion of Palm Sunday (Tone 4, *Buried with Thee through baptism, O Christ our God...* / *Спогре́бшеся тебѣ̀ Кр҃ще́нїемъ*), emphasizing the mystical union of the believer's baptism with Christ's death, burial, and triumphant resurrection.
[^89]: Psalm 92:1–2, 5 (LXX): The Great Prokeimenon of Saturday Evening Vespers (*The Lord is King, He is clothed with majesty*), sung with its full four verses.
[^90]: Genesis 49:1–2, 8–12: The prophetic blessing of the Patriarch Jacob upon his son Judah before his death, foretelling that the scepter shall not depart from Judah until Shiloh come, binding his foal unto the vine and washing his garments in wine and the blood of grapes—the foundational Old Testament prophetic type of Christ riding upon the foal of an ass and redeeming mankind in His own blood.
[^91]: Zephaniah 3:14–19 (LXX): 'Rejoice greatly, O daughter of Zion; proclaim aloud, O daughter of Jerusalem; be glad and rejoice with all thy heart... The Lord thy God in the midst of thee is mighty; He will save thee...'
[^92]: Zechariah 9:9–15 (LXX): The prophetic announcement of Christ's triumphal entry into Jerusalem: 'Rejoice greatly, O daughter of Zion; proclaim aloud, O daughter of Jerusalem: behold, thy King comes unto thee; He is just, and having salvation; lowly, and riding upon an ass, and upon a colt the foal of an ass.'
[^93]: Matthew 21:15–16: Christ cites Psalm 8:2 against the chief priests and scribes who were indignant at the children crying 'Hosanna to the Son of David' in the temple: 'Yea; have ye never read, Out of the mouth of babes and sucklings Thou hast perfected praise?'
[^95]: The festive Troparion of Palm Sunday (Tone 1, *By raising Lazarus from the dead...*) is appointed to be chanted twice at the Blessing of the Loaves (Artoklasia) at Great Vespers.
[^96]: Folio 10 (leaf p14).
[^97]: Folio 20 (leaf p24), where the second Troparion of Palm Sunday (*Buried with Thee through baptism...*) was printed at Small Vespers.
[^98]: A comprehensive typikon rubric (*Markova glava*, 'Chapter of Mark') detailing the liturgical ordal when the Great Feast of the Annunciation of the Most Holy Theotokos (March 25) coincides with Palm Sunday. In the Byzantine and Ruthenian typikon, such concurrences require an intricate synthesis of festive hymnography, combining the feast of the Incarnation with the Dominical entry into Jerusalem.
[^99]: Melodic model (*Podoben*): *Tone 1, Podoben: "Being called from on high" (Свы́шше зва́)*.
[^100]: Melodic model (*Podoben*): *Tone 6, Podoben: "Having laid aside all earthly hope" (Все оу҆пова́нїе)*.
[^94]: John 12:1: 'Then Jesus six days before the passover came to Bethany, where Lazarus was which had been dead, whom He raised from the dead.'
[^101]: Melodic model (*Podoben*): *Tone 4, Podoben: "Joseph marveled" (Оу҆диви́ся І҆ѡ́сифъ)*.
[^102]: Melodic model (*Podoben*): *Tone 4, Podoben: "Thou hast appeared today" (Ꙗ҆ви́лся є҆сѝ)*.
[^103]: Melodic model (*Podoben*): *Tone 1, Podoben: "When the stone had been sealed" (Ка́мени знамена́нꙋ)*.
[^104]: Melodic model (*Podoben*): *Tone 8, Podoben: "That which was commanded mystically" (Повеле́нное)*.
[^105]: Homilies appointed for reading at Matins: Saint Andrew of Jerusalem (Metropolitan of Crete, ca. 660–740), *Homily on the Palm-Bearing Feast* (PG 97:985–1014); or Saint John Chrysostom, *Homilies on Matthew 21* (Homilies 65 and 66, PG 58:617–632).
[^106]: Matthew 21:1–11, 15–17 (Pericope 83): The Matins Gospel reading of Palm Sunday recounting the disciples fetching the donkey and the colt, the garments spread along the way, and the entrance into Jerusalem.
[^107]: The solemn Prayer for the Blessing of the Palms and Branches (*Molitwa na Blahoslovenïe Baïa*), chanted by the priest while censing the branches in the form of a cross, invoking the divine blessing upon those who bear these symbols of Christ's victory over death and corruption.
[^108]: In Ruthenian and Slavic practice, the distribution of blessed palm and willow branches takes place immediately after the veneration of the Holy Gospel at Matins, while the choir chants Psalm 50 and the festal idiomela.
[^109]: The Canon of Palm Sunday, an acrostic masterwork composed by Saint Cosmas the Melodist (Bishop of Maiuma, 8th century). The acrostic in the original Greek reads: Ὡσαννὰ Χριστῷ εὐλογημένῳ θεῷ ('Hosanna unto Christ the Blessed God'), reflected in the Slavonic letters noted in the margin of each stanza.
[^110]: Exodus 15:8 (LXX): The parting of the Red Sea, the foundational Old Testament canticle theme for Ode 1 of the canon.
[^111]: Exodus 17:6 (LXX) and Numbers 20:11 (LXX): The miraculous drawing of water from the flinty rock in Horeb struck by Moses; interpreted by Saint Cosmas as a type of Christ, the spiritual Rock (1 Corinthians 10:4).
[^112]: Habakkuk 3:3 (LXX): 'God shall come from Teman, and the Holy One from mount Paran, a shaded and dense mountain. His glory covered the heavens, and the earth was full of His praise.' Scriptural basis for Ode 4.
[^113]: Isaiah 40:9 (LXX): 'O thou that bringest good tidings to Zion, go up into the high mountain; O thou that bringest good tidings to Jerusalem, lift up thy voice with strength; lift it up, be not afraid.' Scriptural basis for Ode 5.
[^114]: Jonah 2:2–9 (LXX): The prayer of Jonah from the belly of the sea-monster, combined with the sealing of the New Covenant in Christ's precious blood (Hebrews 9:15–20; Matthew 26:28). Scriptural basis for Ode 6.
[^115]: Isaiah 9:2 (LXX) and Matthew 4:16: 'The people that sat in darkness have seen a great light; and to them that sat in the region and shadow of death, light is dawned.' Canticle theme for Ode 6.
[^116]: Zechariah 9:11 (LXX): 'And Thou by the blood of Thy covenant hast sent forth Thy prisoners out of the pit that has no water.'
[^117]: The Kontakion of Palm Sunday, Tone 6, attributed to Saint Romanos the Melodist (6th century), expressing the paradox of the heavenly King seated upon an earthly colt.
[^118]: The Ikos of Palm Sunday, contrasting the slaughter of the Holy Innocents by Herod (Matthew 2:16) with Christ's voluntary self-offering on the Cross for all humanity.
[^119]: The Synaxarion of Palm Sunday (the Master's Feast of the Entry of the Lord into Jerusalem), translated from the Byzantine Triodion compiled by Nikephoros Kallistos Xanthopoulos (c. 1320), presenting the scriptural and theological significance of the feast.
[^120]: John 12:1–3: The supper at Bethany in the house of Simon the leper, where Lazarus reclined at table and Mary anointed the feet of Jesus with costly spikenard.
[^121]: Matthew 21:1–9; Mark 11:1–10; Luke 19:28–38; John 12:12–15.
[^122]: Greek βάϊον (*baïon*), loanword from Coptic/Egyptian *bai* (palm branch), rendered *vaïa* in Slavonic and Ruthenian tradition.
[^123]: Zechariah 9:9 (LXX): 'Rejoice greatly, O daughter of Zion; proclaim it, O daughter of Jerusalem: behold, thy King comes to thee, just and having salvation; He is meek and riding upon an ass, and a young foal.'
[^124]: Psalm 8:2 (LXX 8:3); Matthew 21:16.
[^125]: Daniel 3 (LXX): The Three Holy Youths (Ananias, Azarias, and Misael) preserved unburned in the Babylonian furnace, while the Chaldeans who cast them in were consumed by the flames; the canticle theme for Ode 7.
[^126]: Daniel 3:57 (LXX): 'O all ye works of the Lord, bless ye the Lord; praise and exalt Him above all forever.' Scriptural basis for Ode 8.
[^127]: Psalm 32:17 (LXX) and Zechariah 9:9–10 (LXX): The destruction of chariot and warhorse, replaced by the peaceful humility of the King riding upon an ass's colt.
[^128]: Jeremiah 7:11 (LXX) and Matthew 21:13: 'My house shall be called the house of prayer; but ye have made it a den of thieves.'
[^129]: On Great Feasts of the Lord, the Magnificat (*Chestneyshuyu* / 'More honorable than the Cherubim') is replaced at Ode 9 by the festal refrain (*Pripiv*) and the Heirmos.
[^130]: Psalm 117:26–27 (LXX): 'God is the Lord, and He hath appeared unto us: establish the feast with thick branches, even unto the horns of the altar.' Scriptural basis for Ode 9.
[^131]: Psalm 2:1 (LXX): 'Why have the heathen raged, and the peoples meditated vain things?' Applied to the Sanhedrin questioning the identity of Jesus (Matthew 21:10).
[^132]: Baruch 3:36–38 (LXX): 'This is our God, and there shall none other be accounted of in comparison of Him. He hath found out all the way of knowledge, and hath given it unto Jacob His servant, and to Israel His beloved. Afterward did He show Himself upon earth, and conversed with men.'
[^133]: Isaiah 59:7–8 (LXX) and Romans 3:15: 'Their feet run to evil, and they are swift to shed innocent blood: their thoughts are thoughts of iniquity.'
[^134]: Rubric indicating that on Palm Sunday the Exapostilarion (Photogogikon) is omitted, proceeding straight to *Svyat Gospod Boh nash* ('Holy is the Lord our God') in Tone 4, followed immediately by the Praises (Lauds).
[^135]: Isaiah 6:1–5 (LXX) and John 12:41: Isaiah's vision of the Lord of Hosts enthroned upon high, interpreted patristically as the pre-incarnate glory of Christ.
[^136]: Matthew 26:17–18; Mark 14:12–14; Luke 22:7–11.
[^137]: The Troparion of Lazarus Saturday and Palm Sunday (*Obshcheye voskreseniye* / 'Confirming the common resurrection'), located at folio 10 (physical leaf p14).
[^138]: A solemn procession (*Litia*) around the monastic enclosure carrying palms and branches, followed by the monastic chapter reading from the Catecheses of Saint Theodore the Studite (*Oglasheniye Studitovo*).
[^139]: The Divine Liturgy of Palm Sunday features the Festal Antiphons drawn from the Egyptian Hallel (Psalms 114, 115, and 117 LXX), with proper refrains celebrating Christ's entry seated upon the colt of an ass.
[^140]: The second Troparion of Palm Sunday (*Spohrebshasya Tebi*), based on Romans 6:4 and Colossians 2:12.
[^141]: Typikon dispensation (*razresheniye*): On Palm Sunday, despite the Great Fast, the rule permits the consumption of fish, wine, and oil (*yamy bo ryby, i vino piyem*), celebrating the Master's Feast before entering the strict fast of Great and Holy Week.
[^142]: Palm Sunday Vespers marks the solemn liturgical bridge between the festive triumph of the Entry into Jerusalem and the sorrowful journey of Great and Holy Passion Week, introducing the themes of the Bridegroom, the Barren Fig Tree, and the Patriarch Joseph.
[^143]: The celebrated apostichon of Palm Sunday Vespers (*Ot vetviy i vaïa*), setting the solemn key for entrance into Passion Week.
[^144]: Hebrews 10:31 and Hebrews 4:12.
[^145]: Hebrews 4:16.
[^146]: Hosea 2:2–5; Jeremiah 3:1–9; Matthew 21:15–16.
[^147]: Typikon rubric noting that the Lenten penitential dismissal troparia (*Bohoroditse Divo, raduysya* with deep prostrations) are chanted at Palm Sunday Vespers for the last time; during Holy Week itself, the Bridegroom order supersedes them.
[^148]: Detailed liturgical chapter (*Markova glava* / Typikon chapter) governing the concurrence (*kyriopascha* / coincidence) when the Great Feast of the Annunciation of the Theotokos (March 25) falls on Great and Holy Monday.
[^149]: An ancient rigorist fasting rubric of the Ruthenian and Byzantine Typikon: whereas on Palm Sunday and the Annunciation outside of Holy Week fish is permitted, if the Annunciation coincides with Great Week (even on Great Monday, Tuesday, or Wednesday), fish is strictly forbidden, permitting only wine and olive oil.
[^150]: Saint Andrew of Crete (c. 660–740), author of the Great Canon, who also composed the Compline Triodes for the first three days of Holy Week.
[^151]: Liturgical rule governing the Afterfeast (*Otdaniye* / Apodosis): because Holy Week focuses exclusively on the Lord's Passion, ordinary festal after-feasts are suppressed, except within a temple dedicated to the Annunciation or Archangel Gabriel.
[^152]: The Holy Week distribution of the Psalter: during the first three days of Great Week, the entire Psalter (20 Kathismata) is read through once, concluding at the Sixth Hour of Great Wednesday, after which all ordinary psalmody ceases in anticipation of the Mystical Supper, Passion, and Burial.
[^153]: The Compline Triode (*Tripesnets*) of Great and Holy Monday, composed by Saint Andrew of Crete (Odes 1, 8, and 9), combining the themes of the Patriarch Joseph as a type of Christ and the barren fig tree cursed by the Lord (Matthew 21:18–22).
[^154]: Exodus 15:1–3 (LXX): Canticle of Moses at the Red Sea, the foundational Heirmos theme for Ode 1.
[^155]: Genesis 39: The chastity of Joseph resisting the solicitations of Potiphar's wife, celebrated throughout Holy Week as the icon of spiritual purity.
[^156]: Matthew 21:19: The barren fig tree having leaves only, interpreted as the hypocritical assembly of the Pharisees devoid of righteous fruit.
